        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:exp-setup}\n\nWe evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across six models on three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations across ${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances. Beyond accuracy comparisons, we conduct diagnostic analyses of symbolic confirmation bias and process-level trace independence (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Models.} Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen3-14B (non-thinking mode)~\\citep{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\\citep{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{dubey2024llama3}, and QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwen2024qwq} (7B--32B parameters).\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\\citep{han2022folio} ($n{=}204$, with gold logical annotations), ProofWriter depth-5~\\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter} ($n{=}600$), LogiQA~\\citep{liu2020logiqa} ($n{=}200$). StrategyQA~\\citep{geva2021strategyqa} ($n{=}200$) serves as a domain-formalizability diagnostic. GSM8K~\\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} ($n{=}30$) and AIME 2024 ($n{=}22$) probe the degenerate regime ($C_0 < 5\\%$).\n\n\\paragraph{Parameters.} $K{=}12$ traces at $T{=}0.7$; extraction at $T_{\\text{ext}}{=}0.3$. Z3 timeout: 10s. $\\alpha{=}0.5$. Statistical significance via McNemar exact test ($\\alpha{=}0.05$); 95\\% bootstrap CIs from 10,000 resamples for $n \\geq 200$.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational cost.} On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($K{=}12$), SICA takes $4.5{\\times}$ SC wall-clock time; constraint extraction ($K$ additional LLM calls) accounts for 76\\% of overhead, Z3 solving is negligible (${<}0.1\\%$).\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports accuracy across all conditions sorted by SC baseline. We identify three empirical regimes (thresholds observation-driven, not theoretically derived). SICA effectiveness depends sharply on this accuracy regime.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{SICA vs.\\ SC across all conditions sorted by SC accuracy. Results hold across the full capability range: from Mistral-7B (54.41\\% SC) to Qwen3-14B thinking mode (86.27\\% SC), SICA delivers no significant gain. $\\Delta$ = SICA $-$ SC (pp). $^b$In-context learning with gold logical examples. $\\dagger$\\,$n < 100$ (suggestive only). \\textbf{Bold $\\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$ (McNemar). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$;\\enspace{**}\\,$p < 0.01$;\\enspace{***}\\,$p < 0.001$. Failure modes detailed in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}. $^{4s}$\\,4-seed replication (confirmatory); unmarked single-seed conditions are exploratory.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Domain & $n$ & SC (\\%) & SICA (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Intermediate SC (SC $\\sim$50--55\\%, Mistral-7B only)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 200 & 51.00 & 51.50 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s123) & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\\\\nMistral-7B$^{4s}$ & FOLIO & 204 & 55.88 & 59.31 & $+$3.43 & 0.092 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 54.41 & 53.92 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%)}} \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 75.17 & 72.67 & $\\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & 74.51 & $-$0.98 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 78.50 & 78.00 & $-$0.50 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 84.80 & 84.31 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.00 & 85.67 & $+$0.67 & 1.000 \\\\\nQwen3-14B (think) & FOLIO & 204 & 86.27 & 86.76 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Degenerate ($C_0 < 5\\%$)}} \\\\\nQwQ-32B & AIME 2024$^\\dagger$ & 22 & 72.70 & 72.70 & 0.00 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\\dagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & \\textrm{---} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold logical annotations + Z3)}} \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 55.88 & \\textbf{85.78} & $\\mathbf{+29.90}$ & $1.0{\\times}10^{-13}${***} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & \\textbf{89.71} & $\\mathbf{+14.22}$ & $2.0{\\times}10^{-7}${***} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & \\textbf{86.67} & $\\mathbf{+11.50}$ & $9.21{\\times}10^{-15}${***} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nNone of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect. Two show significant degradation: Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$) and Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter ($-2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$). The intermediate-SC region (50--55\\%) contains the only positive trends, none reaching $p < 0.05$. TOST equivalence tests \\citep{lakens2017equivalence} ($\\pm 2$\\,pp) establish equivalence for 4 conditions; 5 show genuine differences (4 negative); 6 are underpowered (Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}). We trace the failure to three mechanisms in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control}\n\\label{sec:oracle}\n\nOracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: logical formulae from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA yields $+29.90$\\,pp on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($p < 10^{-13}$), $+14.22$\\,pp on Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO ($p = 2.0 \\times 10^{-7}$), and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 10^{-14}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\\% (ProofWriter) of problems. The MAX-SAT mechanism is sound; Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\\,pp) captures only 11.5\\% of its oracle ceiling, with ${\\sim}$88\\% lost to extraction quality.\n\n\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KU9ki3erubw5HTuHpTtMcH`</summary>

```text
18	
19	\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\{t_1, \ldots, t_K\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.
20	
21	\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}). The \emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.
22	
23	\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:
24	\begin{equation}

The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces.     "originalFile": "\\section{Background \\& Setup}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. Note that $\\kappa$ is a qualitative proxy for the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC); we use $K_{\\text{eff}}$ as an interpretive indicator rather than a precise statistical estimator. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts logical constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC. The penalty weight $\\alpha = 0.5$ is not a critical hyperparameter: accuracy varies by at most $0.49$\\,pp across $\\alpha \\in \\{0.0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract logical\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}). We quantify mirage intensity via the \\emph{Bias Ratio}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\n\\text{BR}(M,T) = \\frac{P(c \\text{ supports } a_M \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)}{P(c \\text{ supports } a^* \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$ (\\emph{direct}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Together with the oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}), BR bounds improvement: self-extracted constraints capture at most a $1/\\text{BR}$ fraction of the oracle ceiling ($\\Delta_{\\max} \\lesssim 6.0$\\,pp for Mistral-7B, $\\lesssim 3.6$\\,pp for Qwen-14B; observed mean across conditions: $+2.12$\\,pp). If chain-of-thought traces are unfaithful representations of the model's actual reasoning process \\citep{turpin2024language}, self-extracted constraints inherit representation bias rather than reasoning content---making the mirage an inherent property of extraction from opaque models, not merely a quality issue. Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below $1.0$ (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to CoT faithfulness.} BR operationalizes a specific form of unfaithful reasoning: the model's symbolic output reflects its answer rather than the problem's logical structure. \\citet{turpin2024language} demonstrate that chain-of-thought explanations can be systematically unfaithful---models produce plausible reasoning that does not reflect their actual decision process. Our bias ratio extends this observation from natural-language explanations to formal symbolic outputs: even when constrained to produce Z3-compatible logical formulae, the model generates constraints that post-hoc rationalize its answer ($\\text{BR} = 4.97\\times$) rather than independently verify it. The formalization step thus provides a veneer of rigor without escaping the faithfulness problem.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Background \\& Setup}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. Note that $\\kappa$ is a qualitative proxy for the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC); we use $K_{\\text{eff}}$ as an interpretive indicator rather than a precise statistical estimator. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts logical constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC. The penalty weight $\\alpha = 0.5$ is not a critical hyperparameter: accuracy varies by at most $0.49$\\,pp across $\\alpha \\in \\{0.0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}). We quantify mirage intensity via the \\emph{Bias Ratio}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\n\\text{BR}(M,T) = \\frac{P(c \\text{ supports } a_M \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)}{P(c \\text{ supports } a^* \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$ (\\emph{direct}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Together with the oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}), BR bounds improvement: self-extracted constraints capture at most a $1/\\text{BR}$ fraction of the oracle ceiling ($\\Delta_{\\max} \\lesssim 6.0$\\,pp for Mistral-7B, $\\lesssim 3.6$\\,pp for Qwen-14B; observed mean across conditions: $+2.12$\\,pp). If chain-of-thought traces are unfaithful representations of the model's actual reasoning process \\citep{turpin2024language}, self-extracted constraints inherit representation bias rather than reasoning content---making the mirage an inherent property of extraction from opaque models, not merely a quality issue. Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below $1.0$ (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to CoT faithfulness.} BR operationalizes a specific form of unfaithful reasoning: the model's symbolic output reflects its answer rather than the problem's logical structure. \\citet{turpin2024language} demonstrate that chain-of-thought explanations can be systematically unfaithful---models produce plausible reasoning that does not reflect their actual decision process. Our bias ratio extends this observation from natural-language explanations to formal symbolic outputs: even when constrained to produce Z3-compatible logical formulae, the model generates constraints that post-hoc rationalize its answer ($\\text{BR} = 4.97\\times$) rather than independently verify it. The formalization step thus provides a veneer of rigor without escaping the faithfulness problem.\n",

54	\label{eq:process-sim}
55	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),

57	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nSelf-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average, confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}).\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts symbolic constraints consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to weighted majority voting. Confirmation bias generalizes across families: $4.97\\times$ (Mistral), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA), all exceeding $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Bias intensifies along reasoning steps: late-step constraints (step $\\geq 7$) exhibit 27.2\\% conclusion leakage vs.\\ 22.0\\% for early steps (Fisher $p = 3 \\times 10^{-6}$); SICA restricted to late-only constraints degrades by $-10.3$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nTwenty-five-plus remediation strategies all produce neutral or negative results. At the prompt level, nine strategies fail: contrastive prompting ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp), cross-model extraction ($\\pm 1$\\,pp), multi-temperature ($\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp; Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}). At the method level, ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$), Contrastive ($-9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($-18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}). Fifteen further experiments produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Figure~\\ref{fig:interventions-summary}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_app_interventions_summary.pdf}\n\\caption{Summary of 22+ intervention strategies grouped by category. All produce neutral-to-negative accuracy changes relative to Self-Consistency. The grey band indicates $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure}",

          " \\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),",
          " \\end{equation}",
          "-where $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.",
          "+where $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Appendix Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.",

60	\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}
61	\label{sec:diagnostics}
62	
63	Further diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}).
64	
65	
66	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
67	\label{sec:remediation}
68	\label{sec:interventions}
69	
70	Twenty-five-plus remediation strategies all produce neutral or negative results. At the prompt level, nine strategies fail: contrastive prompting ($\Delta = -1.1$\,pp), cross-model extraction ($\pm 1$\,pp), multi-temperature ($\Delta = 0.00$\,pp; Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}). At the method level, ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$), Contrastive ($-9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($-18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}). Fifteen further experiments produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
71	
72	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}).
73	
74	\begin{figure}[t]
75	\centering
76	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig_app_interventions_summary.pdf}
77	\caption{Summary of 22+ intervention strategies grouped by category. All produce neutral-to-negative accuracy changes relative to Self-Consistency. The grey band indicates $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
78	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
79	\end{figure}

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	The Symbolic Mirage manifests through two channels: the \emph{direct} limits voting diversity ($K_{\text{eff}} < 2$), while the \emph{indirect} collapses MAX-SAT to vote counting (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\times$ Mistral $> 3.93\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\times$ LLaMA) aligns with regime boundaries; thinking-mode degeneration ($\kappa = 0.91$, Qwen3) confirms this scales with capability. For practitioners, $C_0$ with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation will fail. If traces do not reflect actual reasoning \citep{turpin2024language}, the mirage is inherent to extraction. Our extensions of prior negative results \citep{huang2024selfcorrect, stechly2025selfverification}---Effective-$K$, bias ratio, and process-level similarity---provide reusable diagnostics for any trace-aggregation system.
5	
```

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We identify the Symbolic Mirage: self-extracted symbolic constraints project the generating model's biases rather than providing independent verification, explaining why twenty-plus remediation strategies cannot break the same-model ceiling. Oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; a cross-architecture proof-of-concept escapes the mirage entirely. For practitioners, architecturally independent signals remain necessary; detecting the Symbolic Mirage in other self-verification domains is a critical open direction.
4	
```

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias. The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Method.}
8	We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}, classifying (premise, conclusion) pairs into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via weight $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Main result.}
11	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\%) and 55.00\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode yields $+1.96$\,pp at $w{=}1$ and $+2.94$\,pp at $w{=}3$ (both n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\,pp ($p = 0.013$) and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\,pp ($p < 0.001$)---while same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). We report $w{=}3$ as primary; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$); FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. Per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\%) and performs poorly on \textsc{Unknown} (3.5\% accuracy), as NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.
12	
13	\paragraph{Why it works.}
14	The 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). This produces per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \textsc{False} problems (71.43\% vs.\ SC's 38.10\%) where the LLM is weakest, while SC dominates \textsc{Unknown} (68.12\% vs.\ 18.84\%). All three NLI architectures tested---DeBERTa, RoBERTa, and BART (all MNLI-trained)---produce significant improvements on ProofWriter at $w \geq 3$ (Appendix~\ref{app:multi-nli}), confirming the effect is not verifier-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Capability gap hypothesis.}
17	Cross-architecture verification succeeds when the verifier's standalone accuracy exceeds the generator's SC baseline. We evaluate across two generator families: on ProofWriter, DeBERTa standalone (55.00\%) exceeds Mistral-7B SC (39.33\%), enabling $+10.67$\,pp at $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$). On Qwen3-14B FOLIO, where SC (86.27\%) far exceeds DeBERTa (49.51\%), the combo yields $\Delta \leq +0.49$\,pp (all $p = 1.0$). Aggregation gain shrinks with model capability: $+8.01$\,pp (Mistral-7B) to $+0.86$\,pp (Qwen3-14B thinking mode; Appendix~\ref{app:single-trace}). Across all conditions at $w{=}3$, combo gain tracks the capability gap (Figure~\ref{fig:capability-gap}): meaningful improvement emerges only when the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's SC baseline.
18	
19	\begin{figure}[t]
20	\centering
21	\includegraphics[width=0.82\columnwidth]{figures/capability_gap_scatter}
22	\vspace{-3mm}
23	\caption{Combo gain vs.\ capability gap (verifier standalone $-$ generator SC) at $w{=}3$. Meaningful gain emerges when the gap is positive (verifier exceeds generator).}
24	\label{fig:capability-gap}
25	\vspace{-6mm}
26	\end{figure}
27	
28	\paragraph{Cross-generator results.}
29	Table~\ref{tab:cross-generator-nli} extends the NLI combo across two generators and four verifiers. Three of four Mistral-7B verifiers produce significant gains ($p < 0.001$), while neither Qwen3-14B condition reaches significance. The pattern is consistent: NLI escape is strongest when the generator is weak (SC $= 39.33\%$) and the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's baseline. When the generator is strong (SC $= 85.00\%$), even architecturally independent verifiers cannot contribute corrective signal, confirming that the capability gap is the binding constraint rather than architectural diversity alone.
30	
31	\begin{table}[t]
32	\centering
33	\small
34	\caption{Cross-generator NLI combo on ProofWriter depth-5 ($n{=}600$, $w{=}5$). Stand.\ = NLI standalone accuracy (\%). \textbf{Bold $\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$.}
35	\label{tab:cross-generator-nli}
36	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
37	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}
38	\toprule
39	Generator & NLI Verifier & SC (\%) & Stand.\ (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\
40	\midrule
41	Mistral-7B & DeBERTa-base & 39.33 & 55.00 & $\mathbf{+10.67}$ & ${<}.001$ \\
42	Mistral-7B & BART-large & 39.33 & 46.20 & $\mathbf{+7.17}$ & ${<}.001$ \\
43	Mistral-7B & RoBERTa-large & 39.33 & 37.70 & $\mathbf{+6.50}$ & ${<}.001$ \\
44	Mistral-7B & DeBERTa-v3-lg & 39.33 & 45.17 & $+4.83$ & .110 \\
45	\addlinespace
46	Qwen3-14B & DeBERTa-base & 85.00 & 36.17 & $+0.33$ & .830 \\
47	Qwen3-14B & DeBERTa-v3-lg & 85.00 & 45.17 & $-0.67$ & .800 \\
48	\bottomrule
49	\end{tabular}
50	\end{table}
51

    "oldString": "The analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Method.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}, classifying (premise, conclusion) pairs into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via weight $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode yields $+1.96$\\,pp at $w{=}1$ and $+2.94$\\,pp at $w{=}3$ (both n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$) and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$)---while same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). We report $w{=}3$ as primary; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$); FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. Per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\%) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), as NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). This produces per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} problems (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) where the LLM is weakest, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%). All three NLI architectures tested---DeBERTa, RoBERTa, and BART (all MNLI-trained)---produce significant improvements on ProofWriter at $w \\geq 3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:multi-nli}), confirming the effect is not verifier-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability gap hypothesis.}\nCross-architecture verification succeeds when the verifier's standalone accuracy exceeds the generator's SC baseline. We evaluate across two generator families: on ProofWriter, DeBERTa standalone (55.00\\%) exceeds Mistral-7B SC (39.33\\%), enabling $+10.67$\\,pp at $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$). On Qwen3-14B FOLIO, where SC (86.27\\%) far exceeds DeBERTa (49.51\\%), the combo yields $\\Delta \\leq +0.49$\\,pp (all $p = 1.0$). Aggregation gain shrinks with model capability: $+8.01$\\,pp (Mistral-7B) to $+0.86$\\,pp (Qwen3-14B thinking mode; Appendix~\\ref{app:single-trace}). Across all conditions at $w{=}3$, combo gain tracks the capability gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:capability-gap}): meaningful improvement emerges only when the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's SC baseline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.82\\columnwidth]{figures/capability_gap_scatter}\n\\vspace{-3mm}\n\\caption{Combo gain vs.\\ capability gap (verifier standalone $-$ generator SC) at $w{=}3$. Meaningful gain emerges when the gap is positive (verifier exceeds generator).}\n\\label{fig:capability-gap}\n\\vspace{-6mm}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-generator results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-generator-nli} extends the NLI combo across two generators and four verifiers. Three of four Mistral-7B verifiers produce significant gains ($p < 0.001$), while neither Qwen3-14B condition reaches significance. The pattern is consistent: NLI escape is strongest when the generator is weak (SC $= 39.33\\%$) and the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's baseline. When the generator is strong (SC $= 85.00\\%$), even architecturally independent verifiers cannot contribute corrective signal, confirming that the capability gap is the binding constraint rather than architectural diversity alone.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-generator NLI combo on ProofWriter depth-5 ($n{=}600$, $w{=}5$). Stand.\\ = NLI standalone accuracy (\\%). \\textbf{Bold $\\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:cross-generator-nli}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nGenerator & NLI Verifier & SC (\\%) & Stand.\\ (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & DeBERTa-base & 39.33 & 55.00 & $\\mathbf{+10.67}$ & ${<}.001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B & BART-large & 39.33 & 46.20 & $\\mathbf{+7.17}$ & ${<}.001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B & RoBERTa-large & 39.33 & 37.70 & $\\mathbf{+6.50}$ & ${<}.001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B & DeBERTa-v3-lg & 39.33 & 45.17 & $+4.83$ & .110 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen3-14B & DeBERTa-base & 85.00 & 36.17 & $+0.33$ & .830 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & DeBERTa-v3-lg & 85.00 & 45.17 & $-0.67$ & .800 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

64	
65	
66	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
67	\label{sec:remediation}
68	\label{sec:interventions}
69	
70	Twenty-five-plus remediation strategies all produce neutral or negative results. At the prompt level, nine strategies fail: contrastive prompting ($\Delta = -1.1$\,pp), cross-model extraction ($\pm 1$\,pp), multi-temperature ($\Delta = 0.00$\,pp; Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}). At the method level, ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$), Contrastive ($-9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($-18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}). Fifteen further experiments produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
71	
72	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}).
73	
74	\begin{figure}[t]
75	\centering
76	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig_app_interventions_summary.pdf}
77	\caption{Summary of 22+ intervention strategies grouped by category. All produce neutral-to-negative accuracy changes relative to Self-Consistency. The grey band indicates $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
78	\label{fig:interventions-summary}
79	\end{figure}
```

At the method level, Debate achieves the lowest $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$); all other methods cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nSelf-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average, confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}).\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts logical constraints consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to weighted majority voting. Confirmation bias generalizes across families: $4.97\\times$ (Mistral), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA), all exceeding $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). Bias intensifies along reasoning steps: late-step constraints (step $\\geq 7$) exhibit 27.2\\% conclusion leakage vs.\\ 22.0\\% for early steps (Fisher $p = 3 \\times 10^{-6}$); SICA restricted to late-only constraints degrades by $-10.3$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Appendix Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nTwenty-five-plus remediation strategies all produce neutral or negative results. At the prompt level, nine strategies fail: contrastive prompting ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp), cross-model extraction ($\\pm 1$\\,pp), multi-temperature ($\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp; Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}). At the method level, ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$), Contrastive ($-9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($-18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}). Fifteen further experiments produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Figure~\\ref{fig:interventions-summary}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_app_interventions_summary.pdf}\n\\caption{Summary of 22+ intervention strategies grouped by category. All produce neutral-to-negative accuracy changes relative to Self-Consistency. The grey band indicates $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure}",

    "snippet": "1\t\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n2\t\\label{sec:solutions}\n3\t\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n4\t\n5\t\\paragraph{Method.}\n6\tWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}, classifying (premise, conclusion) pairs into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via weight $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n7\t\n8\t\\paragraph{Main result.}\n9\tDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode yields $+1.96$\\,pp at $w{=}1$ and $+2.94$\\,pp at $w{=}3$ (both n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$) and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$)---while same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). We report $w{=}3$ as primary; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$); FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. Per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\%) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), as NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.\n10\t\n11\t\\paragraph{Why it works.}\n12\tThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). This produces per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} problems (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) where the LLM is weakest, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%). All three NLI architectures tested---DeBERTa, RoBERTa, and BART (all MNLI-trained)---produce significant improvements on ProofWriter at $w \\geq 3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:multi-nli}), confirming the effect is not verifier-specific.\n13\t\n14\t\\paragraph{Capability gap hypothesis.}\n15\tCross-architecture verification succeeds when the verifier's standalone accuracy exceeds the generator's SC baseline. We evaluate across two generator families: on ProofWriter, DeBERTa standalone (55.00\\%) exceeds Mistral-7B SC (39.33\\%), enabling $+10.67$\\,pp at $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$). On Qwen3-14B FOLIO, where SC (86.27\\%) far exceeds DeBERTa (49.51\\%), the combo yields $\\Delta \\leq +0.49$\\,pp (all $p = 1.0$). Aggregation gain shrinks with model capability: $+8.01$\\,pp (Mistral-7B) to $+0.86$\\,pp (Qwen3-14B thinking mode; Appendix~\\ref{app:single-trace}). Across all conditions at $w{=}3$, combo gain tracks the capability gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:capability-gap}): meaningful improvement emerges only when the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's SC baseline.\n16\t\n17\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n18\t\\centering\n19\t\\includegraphics[width=0.82\\columnwidth]{figures/capability_gap_scatter}\n20\t\\vspace{-3mm}\n21\t\\caption{Combo gain vs.\\ capability gap (verifier standalone $-$ generator SC) at $w{=}3$. Meaningful gain emerges when the gap is positive (verifier exceeds generator).}\n22\t\\label{fig:capability-gap}\n23\t\\vspace{-6mm}\n24\t\\end{figure}\n25\t"
  },
